Visual witness description of PDF page 17 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A 2D diagram illustrating a horizontal affine line $\ell$ passing through a circular band (annulus) centered at origin $0$. The line has a directional arrow indicating coordinate $t$. The point $z_0$ on $\ell$ closest to $0$ is marked, with a dashed vertical line segment connecting the origin $0$ to $z_0$. The line $\ell$ intersects the annulus in two segments; solid dots mark the endpoints on the inner boundary circle, and open circles mark the endpoints on the excluded outer boundary circle. To the right of the diagram, the following annotations are displayed: "$\|z_0 + te\|_2^2 = t^2 + C'$", "Each half-line contributes a piece of length at most 1.", and "Sampled points are at least 1 apart: at most 2 points per piece."
```


Call a cyclic progression *affine* when its entire sequence of centered representatives $\tilde y(n_j)$ is affine in $j$ as a sequence in $\mathbb R^D$. Its *signature* is the literal word <a id="perturb:signature"></a>$$\label{perturb:signature}
 \sigma=\bigl((c_0(n_j),\kappa(n_j))\bigr)_{0\le j<k}.$$ In particular, the entries retain the identities of the boxes and the integer band indices. Keys are not renamed when passing from one progression to another.

<a id="stmt-perturb-signatures"></a>
<div id="perturb:signatures" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Counting affine signatures). *Let $T_{\mathrm{aff}}$ be the number of signatures realized by affine cyclic progressions, and let $T_{\mathrm{glob}}$ be the full-label word count of [count:global](#count:global). There is an absolute constant $C$ such that <a id="perturb:signature-count"></a>$$\label{perturb:signature-count}
 T_{\mathrm{aff}}
 \le C T_{\mathrm{glob}}\bigl(k(Dq^2+2)\bigr)^3.$$ In particular, <a id="perturb:entropy"></a>$$\label{perturb:entropy}
 \log\max\{1,T_{\mathrm{aff}}\}
 \le O(k^{3/10}\log k)+6\log q+O(\log k)
 =O(k^{9/10}\log k)=o(pk).$$ All implied constants are absolute.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-perturb-signatures"><summary>Proof of stmt-perturb-signatures</summary>

*Proof.* On an affine progression write $\tilde y(n_j)=A+jB$. Then $$f(n_j)=q^2\left\lVert A+jB\right\rVert_2^2
       =\theta_0+\theta_1j+\theta_2j^2,$$ where $\theta_0=q^2\left\lVert A\right\rVert_2^2$, $\theta_1=2q^2\langle A,B\rangle$, and $\theta_2=q^2\left\lVert B\right\rVert_2^2$. Moreover, since every coordinate of $\tilde y(n_j)$ belongs to $[-1/2,1/2)$, $$0\le f(n_j)\le Dq^2/4\qquad(0\le j<k).$$ Put $B_*=\left\lfloor Dq^2/4\right\rfloor+1$. For each $j\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$ and $b\in\{0,\ldots,B_*\}$, compare $\theta_0+\theta_1j+\theta_2j^2$ with $b$. These comparisons use at most $k(B_*+1)\le k(Dq^2+2)$ affine hyperplanes in the three variables $(\theta_0,\theta_1,\theta_2)$. Their joint signs, including equality, determine the complete word $(\left\lfloor f(n_j)\right\rfloor)_{j<k}$ for all realized progressions. [count:arrangement](#count:arrangement), in the fixed dimension three, therefore gives at most $C\bigl(k(Dq^2+2)\bigr)^3$ such words. Allowing all triples of coefficients, without imposing their geometric relations, can only increase this count.

A full-label word $(L(n_j))_{j<k}$ determines the specific second-system boxes and, because $c_*$ is fixed, every outer bit $c_0(n_j)$. Together with the integer-part word just counted, it determines every entry of [perturb:signature](#perturb:signature). Multiplying the two counts proves [perturb:signature-count](#perturb:signature-count); no independence between the two words is asserted or needed.

For the logarithmic estimate, [count:global](#count:global) bounds $\log T_{\mathrm{glob}}$ by $O(k^{3/10}\log k)$. Also $Dq^2+2\le3Dq^2$, so the remaining logarithm in [perturb:signature-count](#perturb:signature-count) is at most $6\log q+3\log k+3\log D+O(1)$. [setup:parameters](#setup:parameters), [setup:q-bounds](#setup:q-bounds) give $$\log q\le \frac{ck}{D}\log k+\log(2k^2)
          =O(k^{9/10}\log k),
 \qquad pk=k^{19/20}.$$ Since $k^{9/10}\log k/k^{19/20}=k^{-1/20}\log k\to0$, [perturb:entropy](#perturb:entropy) follows. The arrangement constant comes from dimension three, so it is independent of the growing dimension $D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-perturb-cyclic"></a>
<div id="perturb:cyclic" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20** (A cyclic two-coloring). *There is an absolute integer $K_0$ such that, for every integer $k\ge K_0$, the construction in [sec:setup](#sec:setup) gives an integer $$N=q^D\ge k^{ck},\qquad c=10^{-5},$$ and a coloring $C:\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z\to\{0,1\}$ for which no progression $n_0,n_0+d,\ldots,n_0+(k-1)d$ with $d\ne0$ in $\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$ is monochromatic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-perturb-cyclic"><summary>Proof of stmt-perturb-cyclic</summary>

*Proof.* For each key $\kappa\in\mathcal K$, take an independent Bernoulli bit $E_\kappa$ with $\mathbb P(E_\kappa=1)=p=k^{-1/20}$, and define <a id="perturb:final-coloring"></a>$$\label{perturb:final-coloring}
 C(n)=c_0(n)+E_{\kappa(n)}\pmod2.$$ Only this finite family of bits is random. In particular, positions with a common key use the very same bit, even if they have different outer colors. We may take $k$ sufficiently large that $p<1/2$.

First consider a progression containing at least $\gamma k$ of each outer color, where $\gamma=1/100$. Fix a target color $b\in\{0,1\}$. Every position with $c_0(n_j)=1-b$ must have $E_{\kappa(n_j)}=1$ for the whole progression to receive color $b$. By [perturb:multiplicity](#perturb:multiplicity), these positions specify at least $\gamma k/4$ distinct keys. This set of keys is determined before the bits are sampled. Thus independence between distinct keys gives $$\mathbb P\bigl(C(n_j)=b\text{ for all }j<k\bigr)
 \le p^{\gamma k/4}.$$ If some key also occurs at a position with outer color $b$, the prescriptions for that key conflict and this probability is zero, which obeys the same bound. There is no choice of a subset of keys to count: all keys requiring a flip are already specified by the progression and the target color.

There are fewer than $|G|^2=q^{2D}$ choices of $(n_0,d)$ with $d\ne0$. A union bound over these choices and the two target colors bounds the probability $R_k$ that any such progression becomes monochromatic by <a id="perturb:rich-bound"></a>$$\label{perturb:rich-bound}
 \begin{aligned}
 R_k&\le 2q^{2D}p^{\gamma k/4}\\
 &\le\exp\!\left(
       \left(2c-\frac{\gamma}{80}\right)k\log k
       +2D\log(2k^2)+\log2\right).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here the coefficient is explicitly <a id="perturb:negative-coefficient"></a>$$\label{perturb:negative-coefficient}
 2c-\frac{\gamma}{80}
 =\frac{1}{50000}-\frac{1}{8000}
 =-\frac{21}{200000}<0.$$ As $D=O(k^{1/10})$, the other terms in the exponent are $O(k^{1/10}\log k)=o(k\log k)$. Hence $R_k=o(1)$.

Next fix a signature realized by an affine progression, and again fix the target color $b$. For each entry $(c_j,\kappa_j)$ of this signature, monochromaticity prescribes $$E_{\kappa_j}=b-c_j\pmod2.$$ Inconsistent prescriptions give an empty event. Otherwise, if there are $s$ distinct keys and $u$ of them are prescribed the value one, independence gives its probability exactly $p^u(1-p)^{s-u}$. By [perturb:multiplicity](#perturb:multiplicity), $s\ge k/4$, and since $p\le1-p$ this is at most $$(1-p)^s\le(1-p)^{k/4}.$$ Two progressions with the same literal signature impose precisely the same prescriptions on the same random variables. They therefore define the same event for this target color and need be counted only once. Consequently the probability $A_k$ of a monochromatic affine progression satisfies <a id="perturb:affine-bound"></a>$$\label{perturb:affine-bound}
 \begin{aligned}
 A_k&\le2T_{\mathrm{aff}}(1-p)^{k/4}\\
 &\le2\max\{1,T_{\mathrm{aff}}\}\exp(-pk/4)
   =\exp\bigl(-pk/4+o(pk)\bigr)=o(1),
 \end{aligned}$$ using [perturb:signatures](#perturb:signatures) and $1-p\le e^{-p}$. Neither union bound requires independence between different progressions or signatures.

By [dichotomy:main](#dichotomy:main), every nonzero-step cyclic progression is covered by the first case or the affine case. The cases may overlap, which only enlarges their combined bound. Thus the probability that any such progression is monochromatic is at most $R_k+A_k=o(1)$. For all sufficiently large $k$ this sum is less than one, so a choice of the bits gives the asserted coloring. All preceding thresholds and constants depend only on the fixed numerical parameters, and the bound in [setup:q-bounds](#setup:q-bounds) is uniform over every prime $P$ allowed by [setup:prime](#setup:prime). A single absolute $K_0$ therefore works for every integer $k\ge K_0$. Finally, $q\ge k^{ck/D}$ gives $N=q^D\ge k^{ck}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transfers"></a>
### Integer intervals and the two growth regimes

The cyclic coloring has a deterministic product extension. The relevant digit is the first place at which the common difference is nonzero; division by the corresponding power of the base then introduces no carry. The related digit construction of Erdős and Turán (Erdős and Turán 1936, 263) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[erdosTuran1936](#ref-erdosTuran1936)] is recalled, with the least-nonzero-digit argument, by Fox and Hunter (Fox and Hunter 2026, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[foxHunter2026](#ref-foxHunter2026)]. We prove the arbitrary-base product needed here directly.

<a id="stmt-transfer-product"></a>
<div id="transfer:product" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Digit product). *Let $N,k\ge2$ be integers. Suppose that a coloring $C:\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z\to\{0,1\}$ has no monochromatic progression $a,a+d,\ldots,a+(k-1)d$ with $d\ne0$ in $\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$. Then, for every positive integer $m$, $$W_{2^m}(k)>N^m.$$ Consequently $W_r(k)>N^{\lfloor\log_2r\rfloor}$ for every integer $r\ge2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-product</summary>

<a id="transfer:division"></a>
*Proof.* For $0\le n<N^m$, write its base-$N$ expansion as $$n=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}n_iN^i,\qquad 0\le n_i<N,$$ and assign the color $$\bigl(C(n_0),\ldots,C(n_{m-1})\bigr)\in\{0,1\}^m.$$ Here and below a digit is identified with its residue modulo the base. Suppose that $a,a+d,\ldots,a+(k-1)d$ is a monochromatic progression in this interval, with $d>0$. Since $k\ge2$, we have $d<N^m$. There is therefore a largest integer $i\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$ for which $N^i\mid d$. Put $e=d/N^i$, so that $N\nmid e$. For every $j\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$, the exact identity $$\left\lfloor\frac{a+jd}{N^i}\right\rfloor
 =\left\lfloor\frac a{N^i}\right\rfloor+je
 \label{transfer:division}$$ holds because $je$ is an integer. Reducing Equation <a href="#transfer:division" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="transfer:division">[transfer:division]</a> modulo $N$ shows that the $i$th digits form a cyclic progression with nonzero step $e\pmod N$. Monochromaticity of the color tuples would make $C$ constant on that progression, a contradiction.

Translation by one now gives a coloring of $[N^m]$ with $2^m$ colors and no monochromatic $k$-term progression. This proves the first assertion. For the second, take $m=\lfloor\log_2r\rfloor\ge1$ and embed the at most $2^m\le r$ color tuples into $[r]$. ◻

</details>

In particular, $m=1$ restricts the cyclic coloring to an ordinary interval of length $N$. The product uses the same fixed coloring $C$ in every digit, for arbitrarily many digits.

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-product-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-product</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#main:intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:intro">1</a>.* Theorem <a href="#perturb:cyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="perturb:cyclic">20</a> supplies, for every integer $k$ above an absolute threshold, such a cyclic coloring with $$N=q^D\ge k^{ck},\qquad c=10^{-5}.$$ Proposition <a href="#transfer:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:product">21</a> gives, simultaneously for every integer $r\ge2$, $$W_r(k)>N^{\lfloor\log_2r\rfloor}
 \ge k^{ck\lfloor\log_2r\rfloor}.$$ The threshold on $k$ was fixed before $r$ was introduced, so it is absolute and uniform in the number of colors. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Growth in the number of colors

For fixed progression length, a separate construction gives a useful bound with a threshold independent of the length. The construction records a coarse interval for each digit, to force midpoint relations to hold coordinate by coordinate, and a squared norm, to exclude nonconstant midpoints. This is the digit-and-sphere geometry of Behrend (Behrend 1946) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[behrend1946](#ref-behrend1946)], used here to partition the entire interval into progression-free color classes.

<a id="stmt-transfer-large-r"></a>
<div id="transfer:large-r" class="theorem">

**Theorem 22**. *For every pair of integers $r\ge256$ and $k\ge3$, $$W_r(k)>\exp\!\left(\frac{(\log r)^2}{64\log2}\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-large-r"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-large-r</summary>

<a id="transfer:norm-color"></a>
<a id="transfer:color-count"></a>
*Proof.* Set $$b=\lfloor r^{1/4}\rfloor,\qquad
 s=\left\lfloor\frac{\log r}{4\log2}\right\rfloor.$$ Both are positive, and $b\ge2$. Partition the digit set $\{0,\ldots,b-1\}$ into $$H_0=\{0,\ldots,\lceil b/2\rceil-1\},\qquad
 H_1=\{\lceil b/2\rceil,\ldots,b-1\},$$ and let $h(u)\in\{0,1\}$ record the half containing $u$. An integer $0\le n<b^s$ has a unique digit vector $x=(x_0,\ldots,x_{s-1})$ with $n=\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}x_i b^i$. Give it the color $$\left(h(x_0),\ldots,h(x_{s-1}),\ \sum_{i=0}^{s-1}x_i^2\right).
 \label{transfer:norm-color}$$ There are at most $2^s(1+s(b-1)^2)\le2^s(1+sb^2)$ such colors. For every integer $s\ge1$ we have $s+1\le2^s$, while $2^s\le r^{1/4}$ and $b^2\le r^{1/2}$. Hence $$2^s(1+sb^2)
 \le r^{1/4}(s+1)r^{1/2}
 \le r.
 \label{transfer:color-count}$$

<a id="transfer:midpoint"></a>
We show that this coloring contains no nonconstant monochromatic three-term progression. Suppose that three equally spaced integers have digit vectors $x,y,z$ and the same color. Their midpoint relation is $$\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}(x_i-2y_i+z_i)b^i=0.
 \label{transfer:midpoint}$$ For each $i$, the three digits $x_i,y_i,z_i$ lie in one half $H_0$ or $H_1$. Each half has diameter at most $\lceil b/2\rceil-1$, so $$|x_i-2y_i+z_i|
 \le2(\lceil b/2\rceil-1)
 =\begin{cases}
 b-2,&\text{if $b$ is even},\\
 b-1,&\text{if $b$ is odd}.
 \end{cases}$$ In particular this absolute value is strictly less than $b$. Reducing Equation <a href="#transfer:midpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="transfer:midpoint">[transfer:midpoint]</a> modulo $b$ forces its zeroth coefficient to be zero: it is divisible by $b$ and has absolute value less than $b$. Dividing by $b$ and repeating proves $x_i-2y_i+z_i=0$ for every $i$. Thus $x+z=2y$ in $\mathbb R^s$. Equality of the final entries in Equation <a href="#transfer:norm-color" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="transfer:norm-color">[transfer:norm-color]</a> also gives $\left\lVert x\right\rVert_2^2=\left\lVert y\right\rVert_2^2=\left\lVert z\right\rVert_2^2$, and therefore $$\left\lVert x-z\right\rVert_2^2
 =2\left\lVert x\right\rVert_2^2+2\left\lVert z\right\rVert_2^2-4\left\lVert y\right\rVert_2^2=0.$$ It follows that $x=y=z$, so the original three integers were equal. This excludes every progression with positive difference and length $k\ge3$, since its first three terms would already give a forbidden triple. After translating the interval by one and using Equation <a href="#transfer:color-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="transfer:color-count">[transfer:color-count]</a>, we obtain $W_r(k)>b^s$.

Finally let $L=\log r\ge8\log2$. Since $\lfloor u\rfloor\ge u/2$ for $u\ge1$, the parameter choices give $$s\ge\frac{L}{8\log2},\qquad
 \log b\ge\frac L4-\log2\ge\frac L8.$$ Consequently $\log(b^s)\ge L^2/(64\log2)$, proving the stated bound for every $r\ge256$, independently of $k\ge3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Uniform growth statements

<a id="stmt-transfer-limits"></a>
<div id="transfer:limits" class="corollary">

**Corollary 23**. *With integer parameters in both infima, $$\lim_{k\to\infty}
 \inf_{\substack{r\in\mathbb Z\\r\ge2}}
 \frac{\log W_r(k)}{k\log r}=\infty,
 \qquad
 \lim_{r\to\infty}
 \inf_{\substack{k\in\mathbb Z\\k\ge3}}
 \frac{\log W_r(k)}{\log r}=\infty.$$ In particular, $W_r(k)^{1/k}\to\infty$ for every fixed integer $r\ge2$, and $W_r(k)/r^A\to\infty$ as $r\to\infty$ for every fixed integer $k\ge3$ and every fixed real $A>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-limits"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-limits</summary>

*Proof.* For $r\ge2$, the inequality $\lfloor\log_2r\rfloor\ge(\log r)/(2\log2)$ and Theorem <a href="#main:intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:intro">1</a> give, for all $k\ge K_0$, $$\inf_{\substack{r\in\mathbb Z\\r\ge2}}
 \frac{\log W_r(k)}{k\log r}
 \ge\frac{c}{2\log2}\log k.$$ Theorem <a href="#transfer:large-r" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:large-r">22</a> gives, for all $r\ge256$, $$\inf_{\substack{k\in\mathbb Z\\k\ge3}}
 \frac{\log W_r(k)}{\log r}
 \ge\frac{\log r}{64\log2}.$$ The right-hand sides tend to infinity. For fixed $r\ge2$, Theorem <a href="#main:intro" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:intro">1</a> also gives $W_r(k)^{1/k}>k^{c\lfloor\log_2r\rfloor}\to\infty$. For fixed $A>0$, Theorem <a href="#transfer:large-r" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:large-r">22</a> gives $$\log\frac{W_r(k)}{r^A}
 >\frac{(\log r)^2}{64\log2}-A\log r\longrightarrow\infty,$$ uniformly for integers $k\ge3$. ◻

</details>

The restrictions on the parameters are necessary: the exact values $W_1(k)=k$, $W_r(1)=1$, and $W_r(2)=r+1$ are established along with finiteness in Appendix <a href="#sec:upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:upper">8</a>.

<a id="sec:upper"></a>
### An elementary finite upper bound

We record a coarse quantitative form of the classical block-and-focus induction; see van der Waerden (Waerden 1998, 142–45) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[vanDerWaerden1998](#ref-vanDerWaerden1998)]. The recursion below proves finiteness directly, involves only previously defined integers, and applies to every positive number of colors.

<a id="stmt-upper-finite"></a>
<div id="upper:finite" class="theorem">

<a id="upper:recursion"></a>
**Theorem 24**. *Define $F(r,1)=1$ and $F(r,2)=r+1$ for every integer $r\ge1$. For $k\ge3$, with $r$ and $k$ fixed, define $$m_0=1,\qquad
 m_{t+1}=2m_tF(r^{m_t},k-1)\quad(0\le t<r),\qquad
 F(r,k)=m_r.
 \label{upper:recursion}$$ Then, for all positive integers $r,k$, $$W_r(k)\le F(r,k)<\infty.$$ The boundary parameter values are exactly $$W_r(1)=1,\qquad W_r(2)=r+1,\qquad W_1(k)=k.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-finite</summary>

*Proof.* The recursion is well founded: construct all $F(r,k)$ at a given length $k$ after constructing them for every number of colors at length $k-1$. Each of the $r$ steps in Equation <a href="#upper:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:recursion">[upper:recursion]</a> then uses finite positive integers.

A one-term progression is a singleton, so $W_r(1)=1$. Any $r$-coloring of $[r+1]$ has two positions of the same color, and those positions form a two-term progression with positive difference. An injective coloring of $[r]$ shows that $W_r(2)=r+1$. Also, in one color $[k]$ contains a $k$-term progression and $[k-1]$ does not, proving $W_1(k)=k$.

It remains to prove the asserted upper bound for $k\ge3$. We induct on $k$, simultaneously for all $r$, with the induction statement that every $r$-coloring of an interval of $F(r,k)$ consecutive integers contains a monochromatic $k$-term progression. The preceding paragraph proves the cases $k=1,2$. Fix $k\ge3$, assume the statement at length $k-1$ for every color count, and fix $r\ge1$.

For the inner induction, a *focus with $t$ colors* in a colored interval consists of a point $f$ of that interval and positive integers $d_1,\ldots,d_t$ such that all the points $$f-sd_i,\qquad 1\le s\le k-1,\quad 1\le i\le t,$$ belong to the interval, and, for each $i$, the $k-1$ points indexed by $i$ have a common color $a_i$, with $a_1,\ldots,a_t$ pairwise distinct. In an interval containing no monochromatic $k$-term progression, the focus itself must have a color different from each $a_i$: otherwise the corresponding points, in increasing order, together with $f$ form such a progression of difference $d_i>0$.

We claim, by induction on $t\in\{0,\ldots,r\}$, that every $r$-coloring of an interval of length $m_t$ containing no monochromatic $k$-term progression has a focus with $t$ colors. For $t=0$ this just means choosing the single point in an interval of length $m_0=1$.

<a id="upper:matching-blocks"></a>
<a id="upper:next-block"></a>
Assume the claim for $t<r$, and put $$m=m_t,\qquad B=F(r^m,k-1),\qquad m_{t+1}=2mB.$$ By translating, we may work with an avoiding coloring $\chi$ of $[2mB]$. Partition it into $2B$ consecutive blocks of length $m$, and associate to block $j$ the word $$w_j=\bigl(\chi((j-1)m+1),\ldots,\chi(jm)\bigr).$$ There are at most $r^m$ different words. Apply the outer induction hypothesis at length $k-1$ and color count $r^m$ to the word coloring of the first $B$ block indices. We obtain integers $b,\Delta\ge1$ such that $$w_b=w_{b+\Delta}=\cdots=w_{b+(k-2)\Delta},\qquad
 b+(k-2)\Delta\le B.
 \label{upper:matching-blocks}$$ Since $k-2\ge1$, we have $\Delta\le B-1$. Thus the next index $$q=b+(k-1)\Delta
 \le B+\Delta\le2B-1<2B
 \label{upper:next-block}$$ is still one of the allocated blocks. Its word need not agree with the preceding words.

The common word in Equation <a href="#upper:matching-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:matching-blocks">[upper:matching-blocks]</a>, regarded as a coloring of $[m]$, contains no monochromatic $k$-term progression, since such a progression would also occur inside block $b$. Apply the inner induction hypothesis to this word. Let $f\in[m]$ be its focus, with differences $d_1,\ldots,d_t$ and distinct arm colors $a_1,\ldots,a_t$. Let $a_0$ be the color at its position $f$; as observed above, $a_0$ is distinct from all $a_i$.

Take position $f$ in block $q$ as the new focus, namely $$f'=(q-1)m+f.$$ For each $i\in\{1,\ldots,t\}$ and $s\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}$, $$\begin{aligned}
 f'-s(m\Delta+d_i)
 &=\bigl(b+(k-1-s)\Delta-1\bigr)m+(f-sd_i).
\end{aligned}$$ The block on the right is one of the matching blocks, and the within-block position $f-sd_i$ belongs to $[m]$. Consequently these points all have color $a_i$ for fixed $i$. There is one more arm: $$f'-sm\Delta
 =\bigl(b+(k-1-s)\Delta-1\bigr)m+f,
 \qquad 1\le s\le k-1,$$ whose points all have color $a_0$. All $t+1$ arm differences are positive, all arm points are in the interval, and their colors are pairwise distinct. Together with Equation <a href="#upper:next-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:next-block">[upper:next-block]</a>, this proves that $f'$ is a focus with $t+1$ colors, completing the inner induction.

At $t=r$, an avoiding coloring of $[m_r]$ would have a focus whose arms use $r$ distinct colors. Its own color would have to avoid all of them, which is impossible in an $r$-coloring. Hence every such coloring contains a monochromatic $k$-term progression, completing the outer induction. In particular $W_r(k)$ exists and is at most $F(r,k)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### References

<div id="refs" class="references csl-bib-body hanging-indent">

<div id="ref-behrend1946" class="csl-entry">

Behrend, F. A. 1946. “On Sets of Integers Which Contain No Three Terms in Arithmetical Progression.” *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America* 32 (12): 331–32. <https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.32.12.331>.

</div>

<div id="ref-berlekamp1968" class="csl-entry">

Berlekamp, Elwyn R. 1968. “A Construction for Partitions Which Avoid Long Arithmetic Progressions.” *Canadian Mathematical Bulletin* 11 (3): 409–14. <https://doi.org/10.4153/CMB-1968-047-7>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A line meeting the band $m ≤ ∥z∥_{2}^{2}",
      "figure_id": "perturb:norm-band",
      "pdf_page": 17,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "perturb:norm-band",
          "exact_quote": "A line meeting the band $m\\le\\norm{z}_2^2<m+1$, illustrated\n for $m=1$.  Solid endpoints belong to the inner boundary; open endpoints\n lie on the excluded outer boundary.  In general each half-line from\n the nearest point $z_0$ contributes ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.05,>=Latex,\n    every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n  \\path[fill=blue!9,even odd rule]\n    (0,0) circle[radius={sqrt(2)}] (0,0) circle[radius=1];\n  \\draw[gray!75] (0,0) circle[radius=1];\n  \\draw[gray!75,dashed] (0,0) circle[radius={sqrt(2)}];\n  \\draw[gray!75,->] (-2.0,0.6)--(2.12,0.6);\n  \\node[above left] at (-1.82,0.6) {$\\ell$};\n  \\node[above] at (2.02,0.6) {$t$};\n  \\draw[blue!70!black,line width=2pt]\n    ({-sqrt(1.64)},0.6)--(-0.8,0.6);\n  \\draw[blue!70!black,line width=2pt]\n    (0.8,0.6)--({sqrt(1.64)},0.6);\n  \\fill[blue!70!black] (-0.8,0.6) circle[radius=1.6pt];\n  \\fill[blue!70!black] (0.8,0.6) circle[radius=1.6pt];\n  \\draw[blue!70!black,fill=white,line width=0.7pt]\n    ({-sqrt(1.64)},0.6) circle[radius=1.6pt];\n  \\draw[blue!70!black,fill=white,line width=0.7pt]\n    ({sqrt(1.64)},0.6) circle[radius=1.6pt];\n  \\draw[densely dotted] (0,0)--(0,0.6);\n  \\draw[gray!75] (0,0.46)--(0.14,0.46)--(0.14,0.6);\n  \\fill (0,0) circle[radius=1pt];\n  \\node[below left] at (0,0) {$0$};\n  \\node[above] at (0,0.6) {$z_0$};\n  \\node[align=left,anchor=west,text width=6.1cm] at (2.65,0.1)\n    {$\\norm{z_0+te}_2^2=t^2+C'$\\par\\medskip\n     Each half-line contributes a piece\\par\n     of length at most $1$.\\par\\medskip\n     Sampled points are at least $1$ apart:\\par\n     at most $2$ points per piece.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A 2D diagram illustrating a horizontal affine line $\\ell$ passing through a circular band (annulus) centered at origin $0$. The line has a directional arrow indicating coordinate $t$. The point $z_0$ on $\\ell$ closest to $0$ is marked, with a dashed vertical line segment connecting the origin $0$ to $z_0$. The line $\\ell$ intersects the annulus in two segments; solid dots mark the endpoints on the inner boundary circle, and open circles mark the endpoints on the excluded outer boundary circle. To the right of the diagram, the following annotations are displayed: \"$\\|z_0 + te\\|_2^2 = t^2 + C'$\", \"Each half-line contributes a piece of length at most 1.\", and \"Sampled points are at least 1 apart: at most 2 points per piece.\""
    }
  ],
